<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/tbl5/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section><p id="p0140">BMJ primarily down-regulated plasma levels of phospholipids, phosphatidylcholine (PC) [PC(20:4_20:4), PC(22:6_20:4)], phosphatidylethanolamine/PE [PE(18:1), PE(18:1) isomer], phosphatidylserine (PS) [PS(17:1)], vitamin D derivatives (25-azavitamin D<sub>3</sub>, 2-alpha-(benzyloxy)-1alpha,25-dihydroxy-19-norvitamin D3), canavalioside, 13-deoxytedanolide and fertaric acid in BMJ + DIO mice relative to DIO controls (<a href="#tbl5" class="usa-link">Table 5</a>). Plasma metabolites, DG/DAG/diacylglycerol 40:1), an intermediate of triglyceride, and PE(22:6) exhibited the highest increase in abundance (&gt;4-fold change) in BMJ + DIO mice (<a href="#tbl5" class="usa-link">Table 5</a>).</p>
<p id="p0145">Several plasma metabolites involved in lipid metabolism were affected by BMJ intervention. The pathway and chemical classifications of BMJ targeted metabolites in DIO mice were identified <em>via</em> Metabolite Biological Role analysis (MBRole 2.0). These metabolites were predicted to function in phospholipid metabolism (i.e., glycerophospholipid, linoleic acid metabolism), glycerophospholipid metabolism/phospholipid biosynthesis (i.e., phosphatidylcholine, phosphatidylserine, and phosphatidylethanolamine biosynthesis), adaptive immunity (B cell receptor, T cell receptor) and adipocytokine (adiponectin, leptin, DG 40:1) signaling were modified by BMJ treatment (FDR≤0.05) as shown in <a href="#fig3" class="usa-link">Fig. 3</a> and <a href="#appsec1" class="usa-link">Supplemental Table 7</a>. The biological roles of these metabolites were identified as nutrients, membrane stability, cellular fuel and/or energy sources (<a href="#appsec1" class="usa-link">Supplemental Table 6</a>).</p>
<p id="p0150">Metabolite and gene interactions were also predicted between differentially expressed hepatic transcripts and plasma metabolites in BMJ-treated DIO mice <em>via</em> Enrichr (<a href="#appsec1" class="usa-link">Supplemental Table 8</a>). Metabolites (Coenzyme A, <span class="font-variant-small-caps">l</span>-carnitine, Acetic acid, Inositol 1,4,5-trisphosphate (I3P), and S-Adenosylhomocysteine) were linked to 15 human isoforms of mouse genes differentially expressed in BMJ + DIO group. Two genes, <em>CPT1</em><span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M7" altimg="si2.svg"><mrow><mi>α</mi></mrow></math></span> and <em>ACOT12</em>, were predicted to strongly relate to coenzyme A, <span class="font-variant-small-caps">l</span>-carnitine and acetic acid (<a href="#fig4" class="usa-link">Fig. 4</a>). Also, <em>Pparα</em>-mediated lipid metabolism (<em>HMGCS1, CPT1</em><span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M8" altimg="si2.svg"><mrow><mi>α</mi></mrow></math></span><em>, SLC27A5</em>), peroxisome/bile acid (<em>ACOT8, ACSL1</em>), insulin resistance (<em>CPT1</em><span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M9" altimg="si2.svg"><mrow><mi>α</mi></mrow></math></span><em>, SLC27A5</em>), DAG, triglyceride and fatty acid synthesis (DGAT2, <em>PLCG1, PLCB1</em>), <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M10" altimg="si4.svg"><mrow><mi>β</mi></mrow></math></span>-oxidation/thermogenesis (<em>CPT1</em><span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M11" altimg="si2.svg"><mrow><mi>α</mi></mrow></math></span><em>, ACSL1</em>), ER stress mediated apoptosis (<em>ITPR1</em>), and immune response, and phospholipid (<em>PLA2G7)</em> targets were predicted to interact with plasma metabolites as well; however, these interactions did not survive multiple hypothesis testing.</p></section></section><section id="sec4"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">4. Discussion</h2>
<p id="p0155">Diet-induced obesity is a major public health concern that serves as a gateway condition to the development of undiagnosed MetS in many American adults and adolescents.<a href="#bib53" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib53"><sup>53</sup></a><sup>, </sup><a href="#bib54" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib54"><sup>54</sup></a><sup>, </sup><a href="#bib55" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib55"><sup>55</sup></a> To address this metabolic state, bitter melon-intake has been suggested as one of the alternative approaches with beneficial effects against MetS. However, studies delineating bitter melon inhibitory mechanisms against MetS analyzing both the transcriptome and metabolome under DIO state are absent; addressing this critical gap in the literature is the focus of our present study. Our findings in this pilot study show that bitter melon partially induces its metabolic effects <em>via</em> coordination between <em>Pparα</em> and circadian rhythm targets to modulate immune responses, and metabolic processes in glucose homeostasis, glycerophospholipid and vitamin D metabolism.</p>
<p id="p0160">Circadian rhythmic control exists in every metabolic process and its dysregulation can disrupt the rhythmic patterns of various metabolic processes leading to the onset of MetS.<a href="#bib51" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib51"><sup>51</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib56" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib56"><sup>56</sup></a> Accumulation of abnormal metabolic signaling such as insulin insensitivity, inflammation, imbalance of adipocytokine regulation, vitamin D metabolism and glucose homeostasis contribute to MetS development.<a href="#bib16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib16"><sup>16</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib17" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib17"><sup>17</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib26"><sup>26</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib27" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib27"><sup>27</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib30" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib30"><sup>30</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib57" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib57"><sup>57</sup></a><sup>, </sup><a href="#bib58" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib58"><sup>58</sup></a><sup>, </sup><a href="#bib59" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib59"><sup>59</sup></a> Circadian rhythm abnormalities are recognized as the overarching biological trigger for major conditions involved in MetS pathogenesis.<a href="#bib51" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib51"><sup>51</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib56" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib56"><sup>56</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib60" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib60"><sup>60</sup></a> Energy sensory proteins, PPARs<a href="#bib51" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib51"><sup>51</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib61" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib61"><sup>61</sup></a> and AMPK<a href="#bib62" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib62"><sup>62</sup></a><sup>, </sup><a href="#bib63" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib63"><sup>63</sup></a><sup>, </sup><a href="#bib64" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib64"><sup>64</sup></a> regulate circadian rhythmic signaling. PPARs heterodimerizes with retinoid X receptors (RXRs), and binds to the respective <em>Ppar</em> responsive regulatory elements(PPREs) on DNA of target genes to regulate circadian rhythm signaling.<a href="#bib65" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib65"><sup>65</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib66" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib66"><sup>66</sup></a> AMPK mediates the phosphorylation and stability of circadian-associated proteins, CRY and PER.<a href="#bib63" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib63"><sup>63</sup></a> In circadian signaling, NAD + biosynthesis and its rate-limiting enzyme, NAMPT, are regulated by clock genes, <em>Clock</em> and <em>Bmal1</em>, by binding to the <em>Nampt</em> promoter.<a href="#bib63" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib63"><sup>63</sup></a> Increased <em>Nampt</em> expression can activate AMPK leading to enhanced SIRT1 activity via upregulation of NAD + cellular levels.<a href="#bib63" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib63"><sup>63</sup></a> Also, inhibition of <em>Clock</em> and <em>Bmal1</em> gene expression occurs via SIRT recruitment to the <em>Nampt</em> promoter.<a href="#bib63" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib63"><sup>63</sup></a> Circadian dysregulation in MetS has shown a strong relationship to aberrant signaling of metabolic regulators, PPAR and AMPK, and their targets.<a href="#bib63" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib63"><sup>63</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib64" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib64"><sup>64</sup></a></p>
<p id="p0165">Notably, several doses of bitter melon have been shown to lower triglyceride, cholesterol and increase adiponectin plasma levels <em>via</em> upregulation of <em>Pparα/γ</em> signaling and its targets <em>in vivo</em>.<a href="#bib27" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib27"><sup>27</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib66" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib66"><sup>66</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib67" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib67"><sup>67</sup></a> Bitter melon activation of AMPK has been commensurated by our lab studies<a href="#bib23" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib23"><sup>23</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib24" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib24"><sup>24</sup></a> and other reports.<a href="#bib68" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib68"><sup>68</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib69" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib69"><sup>69</sup></a> Interestingly in our study, analysis of the hepatic transcriptome after BMJ intervention in DIO mice revealed an increase in <em>Pparα</em> signaling targets, <em>Acsl1</em>, <em>Cyp4a32</em>, and <em>Cyp7a1</em>, that can lead to the activation of other associated signaling such as AMPK, which is normally down-regulated in MetS-related conditions.<a href="#bib70" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib70"><sup>70</sup></a> Furthermore, expression of clock machinery (<em>Cry1</em>, <em>Per2</em>), RAR-related orphan receptor gamma (<em>Rorc</em>) and NAD rate-limiting enzyme <em>Nampt</em> in circadian rhythm signaling was also enhanced in BMJ + DIO mice compared to the control DIO group in our study. Also, <em>Nr1d1</em> transcription was reduced in BMJ + DIO mice. Expression of <em>Cry1</em> and <em>Per2</em> (circadian rhythmic related genes) negatively correlates with cholesterol, waist circumference, visceral and subcutaneous adipose tissue in obese subjects<a href="#bib71" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib71"><sup>71</sup></a> and synchronization of serum Vitamin D levels in diabetes.<a href="#bib72" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib72"><sup>72</sup></a> In line with these studies and this observation, circadian rhythm signaling interconnects the metabolism of lipid, Vitamin D, and cholesterol. Also, these results suggest BMJ upregulates circadian rhythm targets and its effect on these targets are inversely related to MetS-associated factors such as accumulation of cholesterol and visceral adipose tissue.</p>
<p id="p0170">Improvement of fatty acid oxidation <em>via</em> inhibition of <em>Acc2</em> and enhancing <em>Cpt1</em><span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M12" altimg="si2.svg"><mrow><mi>α</mi></mrow></math></span> gene and protein activities has been viewed as a potential therapeutic strategy to treat MetS.<a href="#bib73" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib73"><sup>73</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib74" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib74"><sup>74</sup></a> In this study, BMJ reduced levels of obesity-associated genes (<em>Hmgcs1</em>, <em>Apobr</em>) and plasma canavalioside; whereas, it up-regulated brown-beige (<em>Fam195a</em>, <em>Slc25a20</em>), adipose tissue-associated genes (<em>Dgat2</em>, <em>Rorc)</em> and moderately increased <em>Cpt1</em><span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M13" altimg="si2.svg"><mrow><mi>α</mi></mrow></math></span> expression in the livers of BMJ + DIO mice. Also, the modest elevation of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M14" altimg="si4.svg"><mrow><mi>β</mi></mrow></math></span>-oxidation via <em>Cpt1</em><span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M15" altimg="si2.svg"><mrow><mi>α</mi></mrow></math></span> expression after BMJ treatment may be considered a possible anti-lipogenic effect exerted by BMJ in the liver of DIO mice compared with control DIO mice. Moreover, we observed a modest increase in diglyceride acyltransferase 2 (<em>Dgat2)</em> levels in the livers of BMJ + DIO mice. BMJ intervention resulted in an increase in the transcript levels of Rorc, a core clock/adipose tissue marker (by a ratio of 2.76) suggesting activation of circadian rhythmic signaling in the liver of DIO mice. <em>Fam195a</em> and <em>Slc25a20</em> were also previously identified as beige fat and brown adipocyte tissue-related markers.<a href="#bib75" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib75"><sup>75</sup></a> Though, upregulation of <em>Dgat2</em> gene levels (coding for <em>Dgat2</em> protein-a critical enzymatic step in triglyceride synthesis in WAT) and other adipose tissue-related targets were observed in the current study, reduction of serum triglyceride and leptin levels as well as the stimulatory effect of BMJ on the hepatic expression of beige-brown fat, clock machinery, beta-oxidation and obesity-associated markers further supports the anti-lipogenic role of BMJ under HFD conditions. Interestingly similar to BMJ, it has been reported that 1% green tea also increases <em>Cpt1</em><span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M16" altimg="si2.svg"><mrow><mi>α</mi></mrow></math></span> levels in HFD-fed mice.<a href="#bib76" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib76"><sup>76</sup></a> Though canavalioside has been implicated in adipose tissue function, its role in MetS has not been well studied.<a href="#bib77" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib77"><sup>77</sup></a> Considering that hyperinsulinemia, hyperglycemia and hyperlipidemia contribute to MetS pathogenesis and can develop as a result of dysregulation of fatty acid oxidation mechanisms, it is plausible that natural products similar to BMJ exert their anti-MetS effects largely through the modulation of lipid metabolism. BMJ activation of AMPK in our previously published studies<a href="#bib23" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib23"><sup>23</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib24" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib24"><sup>24</sup></a> and stimulation of PPAR and circadian rhythm signaling-targets in the present study provide more insight that BMJ may serve as a therapeutic agent against metabolic abnormalities diagnosed in MetS patients.</p>
<p id="p0175">Plasma levels of certain species of phospholipids are also significantly modulated in metabolic conditions such as obesity, cardiovascular disease and diabetes.<a href="#bib78" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib78"><sup>78</sup></a><sup>, </sup><a href="#bib79" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib79"><sup>79</sup></a><sup>, </sup><a href="#bib80" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib80"><sup>80</sup></a><sup>, </sup><a href="#bib81" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib81"><sup>81</sup></a><sup>, </sup><a href="#bib82" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib82"><sup>82</sup></a><sup>, </sup><a href="#bib83" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib83"><sup>83</sup></a> BMJ showed its potential to target glycerophospholipid metabolism as evidenced by the suppression of serum PC, PE, and PS levels with the exception of PE(22:6). Previous reports have also shown that levels of lysoPC, lysoPE and lysoPC were restored following intervention treatments in HFD mice.<a href="#bib84" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib84"><sup>84</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib85" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib85"><sup>85</sup></a> This finding in BMJ + DIO mice may be as a result of PC and PE synthesis associated enzyme deficiencies as observed in other reports on phospholipid metabolism impairment,<a href="#bib78" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib78"><sup>78</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib86" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib86"><sup>86</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib87" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib87"><sup>87</sup></a> decreased cholesterol storage in CTP:phosphocholine cytidylyltransferase deficient macrophages,<a href="#bib88" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib88"><sup>88</sup></a> increased insulin sensitivity in skeletal muscle cells of C57BL/6 mice<a href="#bib81" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib81"><sup>81</sup></a> and observed weight loss in HFD-induced obesity in phosphatidylethanolamine N-methyltransferase-deficient mice.<a href="#bib89" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib89"><sup>89</sup></a> Plasma levels of diacylglycerol (DAG) were increased in BMJ + DIO mice, whereas triglyceride levels were decreased (normally, triglycerides can be produced from DAG by enzyme DGAT or via PC by phospholipase C<a href="#bib90" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib90"><sup>90</sup></a>). BMJ-associated high DAG levels may disrupt triglyceride biosynthesis leading to low levels in DIO mice. This is supported by another study wherein a high DAG diet reduced visceral fat, body weight, insulin and leptin circulating plasma levels in C57BL/6J mice.<a href="#bib91" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib91"><sup>91</sup></a></p>
<p id="p0180">In this regard, previous studies have also reported that certain dietary constituents can interfere in the lipid synthesis/storage processes in hepatic tissues under DIO conditions.<a href="#bib92" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib92"><sup>92</sup></a> One such notable study has demonstrated that polyphenolic fraction of Citrus fruit ‘Bergamot’ (<em>Citrus bergamia</em> Risso et Poiteau) has the potential to target the abnormal accumulation of lipid droplets in hepatic tissues of cafeteria diet-induced rat model of MetS <em>via</em> induction of lipophagy (autophagy of lipid droplets).<a href="#bib92" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib92"><sup>92</sup></a> Bergamot-intake induced lipophagy resulted in dispersion/fragmentation of cellular lipid droplets in the hepatocytes, and the stored triglycerides/cholesterol in the smaller lipid droplets become more accessible to autophagosomes which resulted in hepatoprotective effects against cafeteria diet-induced fatty liver state.<a href="#bib92" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib92"><sup>92</sup></a> AMPK was identified as the possible key-player in Bergamot-induced lipophagy.<a href="#bib92" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib92"><sup>92</sup></a> Given that in our study we also observed smaller lipid droplets in the hepatic tissues after BMJ-intake and that AMPK upregulation and apoptosis/autophagy induction <em>via</em> bitter melon-intake has also been previously reported by us and others in certain cancers,<a href="#bib23" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib23"><sup>23</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib93" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib93"><sup>93</sup></a> there is a possibility that the hepatoprotective effects of BMJ are also partly associated with induction of lipophagy. Interestingly, autophagy induction by bitter-melon constituents has also been associated with PPARγ activation<a href="#bib93" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib93"><sup>93</sup></a> (as observed in the present study) which further indicates towards the link between Ppar modulation and possible induction of lipophagy by BMJ.</p>
<p id="p0185">For glucose homeostasis, BMJ stimulated glycogen metabolism and insulin sensitivity (this effect was in line with previously reported studies).<a href="#bib18" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib18"><sup>18</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib19" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib19"><sup>19</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib26"><sup>26</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib30" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib30"><sup>30</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib94" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib94"><sup>94</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib95" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib95"><sup>95</sup></a> Also, BMJ improved insulin sensitivity <em>via</em> increasing levels of <em>Insr</em> and <em>Pik3r1</em> (glucose uptake) and <em>Ppp1r3b</em>, <em>Ppp1r3e</em> and <em>Gys2</em> (in glycogen synthesis). Acute inflammation marker, <em>IL6st</em>-which enhances pleiotropic IL6 signaling that has been implicated in glucose metabolism <em>via</em> glucose transport, was increased by BMJ. IL6 modulates insulin sensitivity <em>via</em> increase in glucose levels <em>via</em> modulation of AMPK and STAT3/MAPK signaling in skeletal muscle.<a href="#bib96" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib96"><sup>96</sup></a> Interestingly, two <em>IL6st</em>-associated polymorphisms (rs715180, rs3729960) have been linked to elevated MetS-associated markers (waist circumference, triglyceride and fasting blood glucose levels) in non-diabetic subjects.<a href="#bib97" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib97"><sup>97</sup></a> In this regard, bitter melon has been previously shown to modulate cell cycle and apoptotic protein levels in pancreatic cancer <em>via</em> PI3K/AKT, MAPK and AMPK signaling pathways that are critical to glucose metabolism.<a href="#bib23" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib23"><sup>23</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib24" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib24"><sup>24</sup></a></p>
<p id="p0190">In the context of MetS, Vitamin D deficiency <em>via</em> metabolism impairment has been shown to play a pertinent role in the regulation of several MetS risk factors.<a href="#bib57" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib57"><sup>57</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib59" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib59"><sup>59</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib98" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib98"><sup>98</sup></a><sup>, </sup><a href="#bib99" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib99"><sup>99</sup></a><sup>, </sup><a href="#bib100" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib100"><sup>100</sup></a><sup>, </sup><a href="#bib101" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib101"><sup>101</sup></a><sup>, </sup><a href="#bib102" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib102"><sup>102</sup></a><sup>, </sup><a href="#bib103" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib103"><sup>103</sup></a><sup>, </sup><a href="#bib104" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib104"><sup>104</sup></a> Both Vitamin D receptor (VDR) and PPARs can heterodimerize with RXR and in this process compete for binding to RXR-their predominant hetero-dimerization partner. After respective ligand binding to either VDR or PPAR and subsequent heterodimerization with RXR, the VDR and PPARs bind to their response elements (VDREs and PPREs) on target genes to influence transcription.<a href="#bib105" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib105"><sup>105</sup></a> Interestingly, there is a VDRE in the PPAR promoter site, thus PPAR could act as a Vitamin D responding gene; this cross regulation of the VDR and PPAR signaling pathways can in turn influence their respective transcription factor/gene targets mRNA levels.<a href="#bib105" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib105"><sup>105</sup></a> Different studies have shown that Vitamin D treatment with 1,25(OH)<sub>2</sub>D<sub>3</sub> increased <em>Pparγ</em> expression in 3T3-L1 preadipocytes,<a href="#bib104" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib104"><sup>104</sup></a> and mice liver,<a href="#bib52" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib52"><sup>52</sup></a> and also increased insulin sensitivity under obese state<a href="#bib99" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib99"><sup>99</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib106" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib106"><sup>106</sup></a>; but its insufficiency impaired <em>Cpt1</em><span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M17" altimg="si2.svg"><mrow><mi>α</mi></mrow></math></span>, AMPK and circadian-related SIRT protein activities.<a href="#bib57" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib57"><sup>57</sup></a> Serum levels of Vitamin D are inversely related with susceptibility to abdominal obesity<a href="#bib102" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib102"><sup>102</sup></a> and A1C levels<a href="#bib100" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib100"><sup>100</sup></a>; the circardian pattern of the serum levels have been also shown to influence type II diabetes in patients.<a href="#bib72" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib72"><sup>72</sup></a> In our present study, BMJ-intervention group exhibited up-regulated <em>Cyp7a1</em> in cholesterol metabolism and reduced 25-azavitamin D<sub>3</sub> and 2-alpha-(benzyloxy)-1-alpha,25-dihydroxy-19-norvitamin D<sub>3</sub>. 25-azavitamin D<sub>3</sub> is a potent inhibitor of Vitamin D3 conversion to 25(OH) D<sub>3</sub> in vitamin D metabolism.<a href="#bib107" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib107"><sup>107</sup></a> Therefore, there is a possibility that BMJ enhances Vitamin D metabolism to prevent deficient vitamin D serum levels (evident by its reduction of 25-azavitamin D<sub>3</sub> plasma levels). Additional studies are needed to further validate this effect of BMJ on Vitamin D metabolism in MetS-associated diseases in the DIO model.</p>
<p id="p0195">Interestingly in the current study, BMJ lowered plasma levels of anti-tumor macrolide/antibiotic, 13-Deoxytedanolide, and antioxidant fertaric acid in DIO mice. It has been reported in previous studies that at low nanomolar concentrations, 13-Deoxytedanolide, a potent plasminogen activator inhibitor (PAI) inducer, exerted ribotoxic stress and cytotoxic responses in lung epithelial cells and promoted apoptosis <em>via</em> MAPK and JNK signaling in HeLa cells.<a href="#bib108" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib108"><sup>108</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib109"><sup>109</sup></a> Also, fertaric acid has been shown to improve antioxidant activity, and inhibit the binding of human LDL to apolipoprotein B, a main constituent of LDL, <em>via</em> its oxidation <em>in vitro</em>.<a href="#bib110" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib110"><sup>110</sup></a> Thus, it is quite possible that the decreased levels of fertaric acid as a result of BMJ feeding may still be sufficient to inhibit lipoprotein oxidation in DIO mice (considering at micromolar concentrations it inhibits more than 60% of human LDL oxidation). However, to date, the biological role of fertaric acid has not been well studied in MetS. Additional studies to elucidate the mechanistic role of the plasma metabolites shown in the present study will be required to understand how the individual and combined effects of these metabolites impact MetS development.</p>
<p id="p0200">Furthermore, studies show that metabolic dysregulation trigger immune response defense mechanisms such as pro-inflammatory, adipocytokine signaling and immune cell recruitment in MetS development.<a href="#bib111" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib111"><sup>111</sup></a><sup>, </sup><a href="#bib112" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib112"><sup>112</sup></a><sup>, </sup><a href="#bib113" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib113"><sup>113</sup></a> Reports have shown bitter melon increased superoxide dismutase activity<a href="#bib114" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib114"><sup>114</sup></a> and attenuated pro-inflammatory signaling in myocardial infarction, mitochondrial oxidation and HFD or other biological agents (ethanol, lipopolysaccharide, bacterial) induced neuroinflammation, and macrophage/mast cell infiltration in mice.<a href="#bib17" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib17"><sup>17</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib26"><sup>26</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib115" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib115"><sup>115</sup></a><sup>, </sup><a href="#bib116" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib116"><sup>116</sup></a><sup>, </sup><a href="#bib117" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib117"><sup>117</sup></a><sup>, </sup><a href="#bib118" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib118"><sup>118</sup></a> Briefly, our results show BMJ intervention has an expansive influence on immunity signaling as indicated by its targeting of innate/adaptive (GO 0045087, GO 0002460), humoral immune (GO 0006959) and general immune activation (GO 0002253) responses to combat pro-inflammatory mediators involved in adipocytokine signaling. Specifically, our study results showed that BMJ leads to inhibition of pro-inflammatory cytokine (<em>Ccl2</em>), and higher levels of DG(40:1) (adipocytokine-associated metabolite). Serum analysis corroborated this observation as BMJ intervention was found to modulate adipocytokine signaling as indicated by reduction in leptin and concurrent up-regulation of adiponectin levels.</p></section><section id="sec5"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">5. Conclusion</h2>
<p id="p0205">In conclusion, our in-depth transcriptomics and metabolomics analysis suggests that BMJ mediates its metabolic effects partly through modulation of <em>Pparα/γ</em> signaling and its downstream targets in circadian rhythm processes to prevent excessive lipogenesis, maintain glucose homeostasis, modify immune responses signaling in diet induced MetS (<a href="#fig5" class="usa-link">Fig. 5</a>). Furthermore, BMJ stimulation of steroid metabolic targets and suppression of inhibitor, 25-azavitamin D<sub>3,</sub> may lead to enhanced Vitamin D metabolism in the DIO murine model. Since BMJ remains a widely consumed nutraceutical worldwide due to its effects that counteract various metabolic-associated disorders, additional mechanistic studies focused on these identified pathways are required to further establish their integrated correlations with BMJ protective benefits against MetS.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig5" title="Fig. 5"><h3 class="obj_head">Fig. 5.</h3>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=9039170_gr5.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/3798/9039170/8c96c6482f1f/gr5.jpg" loading="lazy" height="446" width="788" alt="Fig. 5"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig5/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Scheme depicting Bitter Melon Juice (BMJ) targeted pathways and their implications in Metabolic Syndrome (MetS). The metabolic effect of BMJ in diet-induced obese (DIO) C57BL/6J mice were determined by transcriptomics (n = 4 per group) and metabolomic analyses (n = 3 per group). Transcripts and metabolites differentially expressed in the liver and plasma lipid-phase of BMJ + DIO mice identified several common metabolic mechanisms. In the liver transcriptome and plasma metabolites, BMJ induced expression of genes and modulated levels of metabolites involved in adaptive immunity, steroid (Vitamin D), and glycerophospholipid metabolism. In addition to these pathways, peroxisome proliferator-activated receptor (PPAR), circadian rhythmic, and adipocytokine signaling which are associated with metabolic syndrome (MetS) and associated conditions (shown on the right above) are also modulated by bitter melon. Based on <em>in vitro</em> and <em>in vivo</em> models in the literature, it could be inferred that BMJ may exerts its anti-diabetic, anti-MetS and anti-inflammatory activity and other beneficial health effects <em>via</em> the activation of PPARγ and AMPK signaling.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="sec6"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Data availability statement</h2>
<p id="p0014">Array data is available from the GEO repository (<a href="http://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/geo" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">http://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/geo</a>) under the accession number <a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/geo/query/acc.cgi?acc=GSE175966" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">GSE175966</a>. The raw metabolomics data is publicly available through Metabolomics Workbench at <a href="https://www.metabolomicsworkbench.org" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://www.metabolomicsworkbench.org</a> with study ID ST001911, project ID PR001205 and associated doi 10.21228/M8Z700.</p></section><section id="sec23"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Declaration of competing interest</h2>

J Am Coll Cardiol. 2015;66(9):1050–1067. doi: 10.1016/j.jacc.2015.06.1328.</cite> [<a href="https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jacc.2015.06.1328" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">DOI</a>] [<a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26314534/" class="usa-link">PubMed</a>] [<a href="https://scholar.google.com/scholar_lookup?journal=J%C2%A0Am%20Coll%20Cardiol&amp;title=The%20CardioMetabolic%20health%20alliance:%20working%20toward%20a%20new%20Care%20model%20for%20the%20metabolic%20syndrome&amp;author=L.S.%20Sperling&amp;author=J.I.%20Mechanick&amp;author=I.J.%20Neeland&amp;volume=66&amp;issue=9&amp;publication_year=2015&amp;pages=1050-1067&amp;pmid=26314534&amp;doi=10.1016/j.jacc.2015.06.1328&amp;" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Google Scholar</a>]</li>

<section id="_adsm93_" lang="en" class="supplementary-materials"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Supplementary Materials</h3>
<section class="sm xbox font-sm" id="db_ds_supplementary-material1_reqid_"><div class="caption p"><span>Multimedia component 1</span></div>
<div class="media p" title="Multimedia component 1"><div class="caption">
<a href="/articles/instance/9039170/bin/mmc1.docx" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" class="usa-link">mmc1.docx</a><sup> (146.6KB, docx) </sup>
</div></div></section></section><section id="_adda93_" lang="en" class="data-availability-statement"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Data Availability Statement</h3>
<p>Array data is available from the GEO repository (<a href="http://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/geo" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">http://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/geo</a>) under the accession number <a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/geo/query/acc.cgi?acc=GSE175966" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">GSE175966</a>. The raw metabolomics data is publicly available through Metabolomics Workbench at <a href="https://www.metabolomicsworkbench.org" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://www.metabolomicsworkbench.org</a> with study ID ST001911, project ID PR001205 and associated doi 10.21228/M8Z700.</p></section></section></section><footer class="p courtesy-note font-secondary font-sm text-center"><hr class="headless">
<p>Articles from Journal of Traditional and Complementary Medicine are provided here courtesy of <strong>Elsevier</strong></p></footer></section></article>
